DOS-BUD's entry names both new rows. What is missing is the finding's *"test that would have caught it"*: `grep -rn "TESTING.md" tests/ src/ --include=*.rs` returns four doc-comment references and **no guard** — the ~15-line id-extraction test was not written, so the catalogue is still the hand-maintained list that has drifted four times in this milestone ([M10-R59](#m10-r59)). |

Restored → both green. Round 8's result reproduces exactly. |

### The budget, attacked a sixth time

**1 · Is there a rendering the published table does not list that costs more than 29 units?** Yes, and
they are not exotic. Brute force over 648 generated renderings (four families × eighteen group
patterns × three separators × trunk/non-trunk × six digit sets), plus a hand list of every French
area code:

| rendering | units | masks whole? | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `01 23 45 67 89` (FR Paris) | 29 | yes | **the published maximum** |
| `03 20 12 34 56` (FR Lille) | 29 | yes | |
| `07 89 01 23 45` (FR mobile) | **31** | yes | |
| `67 22 33 44 55` (LV shape, five groups) | **31** | yes | |
| `02 40 12 34 56` · `04 91 12 34 56` · `05 56 12 34 56` | **46** | yes | Nantes · Marseille · Bordeaux |
| `06 12 34 56 78` (FR mobile) | **46** | yes | *a number anyone might paste* |
| `08 92 70 12 34` · `09 70 12 34 56` | **46** | yes | premium · non-geographic |
| `12 34 56 78 90` | **65** | no | the overall maximum (rejected everywhere) |

The published `29` is the *cheapest* French rendering, and French is the family with the widest
spread. Punctuation matters too, and in the opposite direction: `01.23.45.67.89` costs **1** where
`01-23-45-67-89` costs 29, and `347.123.4567` costs **0** — the universal `+CC`/US recognizer has
`validate: None`, so a US-shaped number is free. That is correct behaviour and worth stating: **the
`+CC` and US arms cost nothing at all**, so no international rendering can reach the allowance on its
own account.

**2 · Is there a legal body that reaches the allowance below 2.6 MB?** Yes — by 3.3×, at the very
rendering the README names. Binary-searched to the exact row; one-column exports of **distinct, valid**
numbers, whole fixpoint, `--release`:

| one-column export | units/row | refused at | bytes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **IT grouped `3XX XXX XXXX`** | **8.00** | **62,500 rows** | **793 KB** |
| LV `6X XX XX XX` | 3.00 | ~120,000 rows | 1.37 MB |
| mixed FR/ES/LV/IT column | 3.25 | ~122,000 rows | 1.54 MB |
| FR TrunkPairs `0X XX XX XX XX` | 1.00 | ~160,000 rows | 2.29 MB |
| ES grouped `6XX XX XX XX` | 1.00 | ~160,000 rows | 1.98 MB |
| IT `347 XXXXXXX` (the cheapest) | 1.00 | ~504,000 rows | 5.77 MB |
| `name,phone` contact export, IT grouped | 8.00 | 62,500 rows | **1.88 MB** |

**And the per-candidate table does not predict any of this.** FR TrunkPairs costs **29** units alone
and **1.00** in a column; ES grouped 26 → 1.00; LV 28 → 3.00; IT grouped 12 → **8.00**. The per-scan
memo is what collapses them, and it collapses each rendering by a different factor. *A per-candidate
cost measured in isolation does not transfer to a column* — one axis further out than the rule R53
promoted, and the reason the band cannot be derived from the table printed above it.

**3 · Does it reproduce through the real binary?** Yes. Release `.exe`, shipped default configuration,
counting mock upstream, OS-assigned ports (55792 / 55791 — neither is 1234, nothing bound to or
connected to 1234), the single proxy child killed by its own PID:

```text
grouped one-column export,  20,000 rows   253 KB -> 200 in  587 ms, forwarded
grouped one-column export,  60,000 rows   761 KB -> 200 in 1332 ms, forwarded
grouped one-column export,  63,000 rows   799 KB -> 400,          upstream contacted: 0
grouped name,phone export,  60,000 rows  1805 KB -> 200,          forwarded
grouped name,phone export,  62,500 rows  1881 KB -> 400,          upstream contacted: 0

positive control "call Mario at 320 123 4567 or mail mario@rossi.it"
  upstream saw : call Mario at [PHONE_1] or mail [EMAIL_1]      raw number / email present: false / false
  client got   : call Mario at 320 123 4567 or mail mario@rossi.it    restored: true / true
```

The refusal body is the actionable per-request message, naming the allowance and the field size and
nothing input-derived. **The fail-closed path is correct in every case I drove.**

**4 · Is 500,000 still the threshold?** That is the maintainer's call, and the *inputs to it have
moved*. R53's closure kept it on two figures: *"an ordinary 367 KB tool result spends ~1%"* and *"the
payload that is refused is a multi-megabyte contact export — rare"*. Both are properties of
`347 XXXXXXX`. On DOS-BUD's own SQL shape with the grouped column: the 354 KB / 5,000-row result
spends **8.0%**, the 3.6 MB / 50,000-row result spends **80.0%** (ARCHITECTURE's table publishes 10%
for that row), and the refused export is **793 KB**. Eight percent is still comfortable; *rare* is the
word that no longer holds. **Flagged as a decision, not a defect** — the behaviour on crossing the
line is correct, actionable and untouched.

**5 · Anything that mints, swallows or drops on the request path?** No, and I re-derived it rather
than inheriting it. `budget.spend()` still has exactly one call site (`recognizers.rs:668`); the only
request-path `Budget` creator is `privacy.rs:113`; every other `Budget::new` / `per_call` /
`unlimited` in `src/` is a `detect` view, a `#[cfg(test)]` module, or `raw_candidates`'s deliberate
`unlimited`. `try_detect` has no default body. `mask_all` propagates with `?` on every pass and on the
M4-R20 confirming `redetect`, and its non-convergence error is `unavailable` — correctly, since it is
the vault's error and not a detector's. `CachingDetector` inserts only after the `?`, so a refusal is
never cached. `FailOpen::fail_open` reads the error, not the budget. Response headers are a strict
allowlist (`server.rs:960-967`) that drops `content-length` / `content-type` / `transfer-encoding`, so
no stale length can be forwarded.

### What I verified independently

- **Suite, both feature sets, no warnings.** `cargo test` → **220 passed / 0 failed / 5 ignored**;
  `cargo test --features onnx --target-dir target/onnx` → **253 passed / 0 failed / 22 ignored**.
  `cargo fmt --check` clean. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean on **both** feature
  sets. `cargo doc --no-deps` → **15** warnings, the pre-existing baseline; M10 adds none.
- **The release-workflow guard, simulated rather than assumed.** Ran `release-build-publish.yml`'s own
  two greps against the current tree: `v1.2.1` → **BLOCKED** (the Status table says `(planned)`),
  `v1.2.0` / `v1.1.0` → would publish. `Cargo.toml` is `1.2.0`, so the manifest guard blocks `v1.2.1`
  as well. Both behave exactly as ROADMAP claims, and the prose-vs-table scoping holds — the guard
  reads only `^\| \[M` rows, so this file's own explanatory prose cannot move it.
- **One mutation** in a throwaway worktree at HEAD `e704ce6`, restored, `touch`ed and the worktree
  removed: R47's guard clause (reds FAILOPEN-BUD **and** DOS-08, and nothing else).
- **DOS-BUD re-run on `--release`**, unit counts reproduced **exactly** (see the verdict table).
- **An independent probe crate outside the repository** — a path dependency on the library with its
  own target directory, so the repo's working tree was never modified — for the rendering sweep, the
  brute-force search, the odometer distinctness count, the binary-searched refusal lines and the
  end-to-end drive of the release `.exe`.
- **Measurement hygiene, applied to myself — and this round that means publishing *no* wall clock.**
  The box was **not idle** at any point: a `copilot` process held ~1.3 cores throughout and a `java`
  process later added ~3 more (sampled 19% → 35% of twelve cores; neither is mine to stop). Every wall
  clock I measured is therefore contended, and by [M10-R50](#m10-r50)'s own rule — *a published number
  is measured on an idle box or it is not measured* — I discard all of them rather than file a finding
  on them. **Every number in this round's findings is a unit count**, which is deterministic and
  load-independent. The one consequence worth recording is stated as a scope claim rather than a
  measurement: see [M10-R56](#m10-r56)'s note on the *"at most about 3 s"* ceiling.
- **Environment.** Every port OS-assigned and asserted `!= 1234`; nothing was bound to or connected to
  1234. The only process stopped was the one proxy child this harness spawned, by its own PID; nothing
  was killed by name. Working tree clean at start and at end; worktree removed and pruned.
- **Things I tried and could not break.**
  - *Reaching the allowance with an international rendering.* Impossible: the universal `+CC`/US
    recognizer has `validate: None`, so `+39 347 1234567`, `+33 1 23 45 67 89`, `(347) 123-4567` and
    `347.123.4567` charge **0** units for their own arm. Their cost is only whatever the *domestic*
    families additionally propose over the same bytes (11–28 units), which those families already
    bound. No international form beats the domestic worst case.
  - *A cache-amplified refusal.* A body of many byte-identical 100 KB fields is charged once and
    replayed free — correct (a hit did no work and returns what a fresh scan would), and it makes the
    *cheap* direction cheaper, never the refusal wrong.
  - *Making a refusal cacheable.* `cache.rs:125`'s `?` precedes the insert, so an error is never
    stored and a later request with its own full allowance cannot inherit one.
  - *JSON-escaped numbers.* The body is parsed before masking, so an escaped number is ordinary text
    by the time a recognizer sees it — no additional candidate shape and no additional cost.
  - *A rendering that reaches the allowance below 793 KB.* I could not find one. 8.00 units per
    13-byte row is the densest column in the shipped set; the FR and ES renderings that are expensive
    *in isolation* collapse to 1.00 in a column, because their sub-candidates repeat and memoize.

---

<a id="m10-r56"></a>
### M10-R56 — the replacement band is wrong in the same optimistic direction: the grouped column both READMEs name is refused at **793 KB**, not 2.6 MB · **measurement**

The claim, live in four sites since `e704ce6`:

- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:317-320` — *"the reachable band for a **legal** phone-bearing body starts at
  about **2.6 MB** for a dense grouped column and about **6 MB** for the cheapest rendering … A
  2.6 MB `SELECT name, phone` export is refused."*
- `src/pii/recognizers.rs:996-998` — *"the reachable band … starts around **2.6 MB** (a dense grouped
  column) and around **6 MB** (the cheapest rendering)."*
- `README.md:216-219` — *"a dense contact export starts getting refused somewhere around **2.6 MB**
  (`320 123 4567`-style grouping) to **6 MB** (`347 1234567`)."*
- `README.it.md:219-222` — the same.

**Measured with DOS-BUD's own grouped generator, that rendering costs a flat 8.00 units per row.**
One-column export of distinct, valid Italian mobiles, whole fixpoint, `--release`, binary-searched to
the row:

| rows | bytes | verdict | units |
|---|---|---|---|
| 62,400 | 792 KB | 200, 62,400 masked | 499,200 |
| **62,499** | **793 KB** | **200, 62,499 masked** | **499,992** |
| **62,500** | **793 KB** | **400 REFUSED** | **500,000** |

That is **3.3× below** the published lower bound, at the rendering the README names by example. A
`SELECT name, phone` export in the same rendering — R53's own headline shape — is refused at
**62,500 rows / 1.88 MB**, not 2.6 MB.

**Repro — the real `.exe`**, shipped default, counting mock upstream, OS-assigned ports:

```text
grouped one-column export, 60,000 rows   761 KB -> 200, forwarded
grouped one-column export, 63,000 rows   799 KB -> 400, upstream contacted: 0
grouped name,phone export, 60,000 rows  1805 KB -> 200, forwarded
grouped name,phone export, 62,500 rows  1881 KB -> 400, upstream contacted: 0
```

**Why the published figure was 2.6 MB.** R53's table 3 reports the same shape at *200,000 rows /
2.6 MB / 500,000 units* — **2.5 units per row**, where DOS-BUD's own generator gives 8.00. A generator
whose candidates repeat is served by the per-scan memo, and 2.5-vs-8.00 is a factor of 3.2. That is
this milestone's oldest trap for the **seventh** time, and the first time it has landed directly in a
published availability number rather than in a draft. The generator is not in the tree — which is
[M10-R58](#m10-r58): the band is an off-harness number, so nothing re-runs it.

**Two figures underneath the threshold decision moved with it.** R53's closure kept 500,000 on
*"an ordinary 367 KB tool result spends ~1%"* and *"what gets refused is a multi-megabyte contact
export — rare"*. Both are properties of `347 XXXXXXX`. On DOS-BUD's own SQL shape, grouped column:

| DOS-BUD SQL shape, grouped column | bytes | units | share of allowance |
|---|---|---|---|
| 5,000 rows | 354 KB | 40,000 | **8.0%** (published ~1%) |
| 50,000 rows | 3.6 MB | 400,000 | **80.0%** (ARCHITECTURE's own table row publishes 10%) |

8% for an ordinary tool result is still comfortable, and the threshold is defensible on it. What no
longer holds is *rare*: a **793 KB** one-column export and a **1.88 MB** `name,phone` export are both
ordinary agent output. **This is a decision, not a defect** — the fail-closed behaviour on crossing
the line is correct, the message is actionable, nothing is forwarded and nothing leaks — but it is a
decision whose stated inputs were wrong, so it is owed a second look before the tag.

**One claim I could not test, and am therefore not filing.** ARCHITECTURE:351-352 and both READMEs say
*"across every shape measured a request costs at most about **3 s**"*. The scope qualifier is doing
real work there, and the shape that maximises **entity count per byte** — a one-column export, ~8× the
entity density of DOS-BUD's SQL row — is not among the shapes measured. My box carried 19–35%
background load throughout, so by [M10-R50](#m10-r50)'s rule I discard my wall clocks rather than
publish them. Recording the *scope* gap only: the two-term model printed beside that sentence
(`validation ≤ budget × per-call` **plus** a linear floor) has no term for the mask rewrite over
~500,000 vault entities, and no measured row exercises one.

**Fix.** Re-measure the band from the harness (see [M10-R58](#m10-r58)) and publish what it prints:
the refusal line for a dense grouped one-column export is **~62,500 rows / ~793 KB**, for
`name,phone` **~1.88 MB**, and for the cheapest rendering **~504,000 rows / ~5.8 MB**. Drop
*"multi-megabyte"* from the threshold justification — *"sub-megabyte for the densest legal column"* is
the honest form and the one that lets a reader judge the threshold. Correct ARCHITECTURE's 3.7 MB
table row the same way its 16 MiB row was corrected: it is 50,000 units in one rendering and
**400,000** in another, and publishing only the first is the defect this table has now had three
times.

**Test that would have caught it.** [M10-R58](#m10-r58) — R54's own fix item 2, the one-column density
sweep, prescribed for exactly this and not built. **The rule to promote**, in
[ARCHITECTURE](../ARCHITECTURE.md) beside the per-rendering table: *a per-candidate cost measured in
isolation does not transfer to a column.* The per-scan memo collapses each rendering by its own
factor — FR 29 → 1.00, ES 26 → 1.00, LV 28 → 3.00, IT grouped 12 → **8.00** — so the isolated table
does not even rank the column costs, and a band cannot be reasoned from it. That is the fourth axis
this milestone has had to learn separately, after `size`, `periodicity` and `shape`: **`context`**.

**Closed (2026-07-30) — read off the harness, in the unit that is actually the limit.** The band is
gone from all four sites and replaced by what `DOS-BUD` now prints. Three things changed at once:

1. **The limit is a count of *numbers*, not of bytes.** At a given rendering the cost per number is
   flat, so the row limit is fixed and the byte limit is whatever surrounds the column. The same
   62,500 grouped numbers are refused at **793 KB** (bare `phone`), **2.0 MB** (`name,phone`) and
   **4.45 MB** (six-column export) — one limit, three sizes. Publishing it in megabytes is what went
   wrong four times.
2. **The figure that matters is the *column* cost, not the isolated one.** Per row in a column of
   20,000: `347 XXXXXXX` **1.00**, any `+CC` form 1.02, FR pairs 3.27, ES grouped 3.27,
   `3XX XXX XXXX` **8.00**. So 500,000 units is ≈**62,500 numbers** at the worst column rendering and
   ≈500,000 at the cheapest.
3. **The harness measures it now.** `DOS-BUD` runs the SQL sweep in **both** renderings, three layouts
   at the refusal line, and a per-rendering column table — so the sixth version of these numbers was
   read off the output rather than typed in, which is what the first five were.

The boundary is exact and worth recording: 62,499 grouped rows spend **499,992** units and are masked;
62,500 spend **500,000** and are refused. Flat 8.00 per row, no rounding.

**The threshold was re-confirmed at 500,000 by the maintainer on these numbers**, not on the ones it
was confirmed against last round. What changed in the justification is the word *"rare"*: an ordinary
5,000-row export spends **8%**, not 1%, and the refused body can be sub-megabyte. That is written into
ARCHITECTURE rather than smoothed over — [M10-R27](#m10-r27)'s rule now has a real counterexample and
the record says so.

<a id="m10-r57"></a>
### M10-R57 — *"1 to 29 units"* is one sample per family, and the French sample it took is the cheapest of its family: six ordinary French renderings cost **46** · **measurement**

`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:302-312`, `src/pii/recognizers.rs:985-993` and both READMEs publish the spread
as **1 to 29 units**, with `01 23 45 67 89` (FR) as the stated maximum. Measured on
`try_detect("call {number} now")`, shipped default, `--release`:

| rendering | units | masks whole? | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `01 23 45 67 89` (FR Paris) | 29 | yes | the published maximum |
| `03 20 12 34 56` (FR Lille) | 29 | yes | |
| `07 89 01 23 45` (FR mobile) | **31** | yes | |
| `67 22 33 44 55` (LV shape, five groups) | **31** | yes | |
| `02 40 12 34 56` (FR Nantes) | **46** | yes | |
| `04 91 12 34 56` (FR Marseille) | **46** | yes | |
| `05 56 12 34 56` (FR Bordeaux) | **46** | yes | |
| `06 12 34 56 78` (FR mobile) | **46** | yes | *a number anyone might paste* |
| `08 92 70 12 34` (FR premium) | **46** | yes | |
| `09 70 12 34 56` (FR non-geographic) | **46** | yes | |
| `12 34 56 78 90` | **65** | no | overall maximum found (rejected everywhere) |

Every 46-unit row **masks whole**, so these are legal numbers rather than adversarial input: the
published maximum for a *real* number is 46, and for *any* candidate at least 65.

**The mechanism is the one R53 named, applied to R53's own table.** The cost of a rejected
sub-candidate depends on which regions accept the *prefixes* the overlapping rescan proposes, and that
varies with the digits, not only with the family. `01 23 45 67 89` happens to produce sub-candidates
that are cheap; `06 12 34 56 78` produces ones that are not. Thirteen rows are thirteen points of a
grid, one per rendering somebody thought of — *a conclusion drawn from one point of a grid is a fact
about that point*, which R53's own closure wrote into ARCHITECTURE eleven lines above this table.

**Two things the measurement gets right and no document states.** Punctuation is not cosmetic:
`01.23.45.67.89` costs **1** where `01-23-45-67-89` costs **29** (the `TrunkPairs` arm admits `.`, so
the dotted form matches one family where the dashed form feeds several). And the universal `+CC`/US
recognizer has `validate: None`, so `347.123.4567` costs **0** and **no international rendering can
reach the allowance on its own account** — a genuinely reassuring fact that is currently unwritten.

**Fix.** Publish the spread as **1 to 46 units for a valid number (65 for a rejected candidate)** and
say what the table is: a sample, not a bound. Add the two 46-unit French rows and the 65-unit row,
note that `+CC`/US forms charge nothing, and drop the implication that the maximum is known — the
honest form is *"the widest spread measured"*.

**Test that would have caught it.** DOS-BUD's rendering table built from a **generated** sweep rather
than a hand list: the same brute force I ran (families × group patterns × separators × digit sets),
printing the maximum rather than asserting it equals a literal. A hand-written list of renderings is
the same artefact as CLI-05's hand-written list of environment variables and `shipped_chains()`'s
hand-written list of chains — **the third hand-maintained list in this milestone to be quoted as a
bound**, which makes the rule general enough to promote to
[TESTING](../TESTING.md#algorithmic-complexity-guards): *a hand-written list may illustrate a bound; it
may never be one.*

**Closed (2026-07-30).** The published spread is no longer "1 to 29". ARCHITECTURE and the constant
now give the **column** figures (1.00–8.00 per row) as the load-bearing ones and note the isolated
range separately — *0* for a `+CC` form (that recognizer has no validator at all), 1 for
`347 1234567`, **46** for `06 12 34 56 78`, 65 for the most expensive candidate found, which no plan
accepts.

**And the two ranges disagree for a reason worth publishing, not just correcting.** FR is the most
expensive rendering in isolation (46) and one of the cheapest in a column (3.27), because the per-scan
memo absorbs the repeated sub-candidate prefixes. *Isolation measures how a rendering behaves; a
column measures what a body costs* — and only the second answers the question the table exists for.
The finding is right that thirteen rows are thirteen points; the fix is to say which grid the reader
is standing on.

<a id="m10-r58"></a>
### M10-R58 — M10-R54's fix had three items and item 2 was not built — the row *"where the 2.6 MB / 6.0 MB refusal line lives"* — so the band is still an off-harness number · **guard**

[M10-R54](#m10-r54)'s *Fix* reads:

> 1. Add a **rendering** axis to the SQL sweep … 2. Add the **density** shape an agent actually
> produces: a one-column `SELECT phone` result at a few sizes, **which is where the 2.6 MB / 6.0 MB
> refusal line lives**. 3. Print **units per number** per rendering …

Items 1 and 3 landed (`tests/complexity.rs:626-658` and `:660-718`). **Item 2 did not.** DOS-BUD's row
inventory at HEAD is: one field at increasing sizes · one request at increasing field counts · the SQL
shape (75 bytes/row) at six sizes in **one** rendering · the per-candidate unit table · the SQL shape
at `MAX_BODY_BYTES` in **two** renderings · the tier-off floor · the tier's share of the cost. There
is no one-column sweep, at any size, in any rendering.

**The consequence is not cosmetic — it is the whole finding chain.** The two rows that *were* added
answer *"is this rendering refused at 16 MiB?"* with yes/no. Neither prints a refusal **line**, and a
line in bytes is what four documents publish. So the band still comes from an ad-hoc probe that lives
nowhere and runs never — and, measured, is wrong by 3.3× ([M10-R56](#m10-r56)). *R54 was filed because
a harness that does not vary the axis its claim depends on will have its narrowest reading quoted as
its widest; the fix varied the axis and still does not emit the claim.*

**The closure note does not mention item 2.** It says DOS-BUD *"gains the axis it held constant: a
per-rendering unit table … and the `MAX_BODY_BYTES` row in two legal renderings"*, and stops. This is
the **tenth** occurrence of *"a closure skipped a step its own finding named"* — M10-R23, R31, R43,
R51 (two), R55 (three), now this — and it happened **in the commit that closed the ninth**, whose own
closure note names the mechanism: *the interesting half of a fix crowds out the checklist half.* The
interesting half here was the rendering axis; the checklist half was the boring density sweep that
produces the number.

**Fix.** Add the sweep — four or five sizes per rendering, printing rows / bytes / units / verdict so
the refusal line is a **row of the harness** rather than a sentence beside it — including the
`name,phone` two-column shape, since that is the one the READMEs describe. Then re-derive
[M10-R56](#m10-r56)'s numbers from its output and publish those.

**Test that would have caught it.** This is the test. The durable rule, to promote to
[TESTING](../TESTING.md#algorithmic-complexity-guards) under DOS-BUD: **every number a product
document publishes must be a row this harness prints.** DOS-BUD already carries *"a number a reader
must trust goes stale; a number they can re-run is a fact"* — what nothing checks is the converse, and
three consecutive rounds have published a figure the harness does not emit.

**Closed (2026-07-30) — the density row exists, and it is where the number now comes from.** `DOS-BUD`
runs the SQL sweep in **both** renderings (six sizes each) and adds a three-layout section at the
refusal line — bare `phone`, `name,phone`, six-column export — so the band is a harness row rather
than an off-harness figure. [M10-R56](#m10-r56)''s replacement numbers were read from that output.

**Tenth instance of the class, and this one closed in the commit that closed the ninth** — which is
the detail worth keeping. R54 had three fix items, item 2 was the density row, and the closure note
described the other two. Not carelessness: *the interesting half of a fix crowds out the checklist
half*, and a note written while the interesting half is fresh reads as complete because it is complete
about the part its author was thinking in. The counter-discipline is mechanical, and it is now built —
see [M10-R59](#m10-r59).

<a id="m10-r59"></a>
### M10-R59 — the tag's stopping rule rests on *"both classes now have a mechanical guard"*, and the catalogue one was never built · **guard**

`docs/ROADMAP.md:1793-1800`, the convergence call the tag decision rests on:

> What has not converged is the loop between *measurement* and *prose* … Both classes **now have a
> mechanical guard** (DOS-BUD's rendering axis; FAILOPEN-BUD and the catalogue entries), which is what
> makes them stop rather than another round.

Neither half is a guard for the class it is offered against.

**(a) FAILOPEN-BUD is not a guard against catalogue drift.** It is an excellent guard — re-verified by
mutation this round — against `FailOpen` swallowing a budget refusal. It has nothing to do with
whether a test is *catalogued*. It appears in this sentence because it is the guard R55 asked to *be*
catalogued, which is the opposite relation.

**(b) "the catalogue entries" are entries, not a guard**, and R55's own prescribed guard was not
built. R55's *Test that would have caught it* is explicit and sized: *"a mechanical guard is finally
worth it, and it is ~15 lines: extract every `#[test]` whose doc comment declares a **guard id**
(`DOS-`, `CLI-`, `E2E-`, `PHONE-`, `CMP-`, `FAILOPEN-`) … and assert each id appears in
`docs/TESTING.md`."* `grep -rn "TESTING.md" tests/ src/ --include=*.rs` returns four doc-comment
mentions and no test. The catalogue is still the hand-maintained list that drifted in M10-R51 (twice)
and M10-R55 (three times).

**(c) And DOS-BUD's rendering axis has the shape DOS-08's first version had.** M10-R42's rule — *"a
guard written to prove a defect is unrepresentable must quantify over the shape the defect can take,
not over the instance that had it"* — applies here exactly: the axis was added at the one point
(`MAX_BODY_BYTES`, refused yes/no) where the last wrong claim was found, not over the shape the claim
takes (a refusal line in bytes, at any size). [M10-R56](#m10-r56) is that shape producing a new
instance in the first round after the fix, which is the empirical answer to *"is this guard capable of
stopping the class?"* — no.

**Why this is filed rather than folded into R58.** The stopping rule is a **decision about the tag**,
and it is stated as resting on a mechanism that does not exist. Round 8's judgement that the *code*
has converged is sound, and this round supports it — rounds 5–9 have now found zero live defects in
`src/`. The part that is not supported is *"which is what makes them stop"*.

**Fix.** Either build R55's ~15-line catalogue guard and R58's density sweep — after which the
sentence is true — or rewrite the sentence to say what is actually in place: two classes with better
*instrumentation*, no mechanical guard, stopped by judgement. Both are defensible positions; only one
of them is what the file currently says.

**Test that would have caught it.** The catalogue guard, on itself. The rule to promote to
[TESTING](../TESTING.md): **naming a guard in a convergence argument is a claim that it exists and
covers the class — check both before the argument is load-bearing.** Twice now (M10-R42's `—`, and
this) a guard has been cited for a class it was not written against.

**Closed (2026-07-30) by building the guard, not by softening the claim.** The finding is right on
both halves: FAILOPEN-BUD guards `FailOpen`, not the catalogue, and R55''s prescribed
id-extraction test had never been written — which made the stopping rule rest on a guard that did not
exist.

**CAT-01** is that test, in `tests/test_catalogue.rs`: it extracts every guard id declared in a
`#[test]`''s own doc comment and asserts each is named in `docs/TESTING.md`, with a ≥ 20-id
non-vacuity floor so a change in the doc-comment convention fails loudly instead of passing on an
empty set. It is deliberately CLI-05''s shape — *extract the claims from the source, assert each
appears in the document* — because that is the one guard in this repo that has kept a hand-maintained
document honest for four milestones.

**It found four on its first run:** `LOG-03` and `PROP-01a/b/c`, catalogued only inside the compressed
forms `LOG-01/02/03` and `PROP-01`. That is exactly how a missing entry hides, and it is the best
evidence the guard is real rather than decorative — the same standard this milestone applied to
DOS-08 and FAILOPEN-BUD.

*(The finding''s point (c) — that `DOS-BUD`''s rendering axis was added at the one place the last wrong
claim was found — is answered by [M10-R58](#m10-r58)''s fix, which spreads it across both renderings,
six sizes and three layouts. Whether **that** is now a grid or three points is a fair question for a
later round, and the ROADMAP names it.)*

<a id="m10-r60"></a>
### M10-R60 — M10-R53's closure narrowed *"four live sites and two narrative ones"* to *"all four live sites"*, and the ROADMAP block still asserts the refuted claim in the present tense · **docs**

[M10-R53](#m10-r53)'s *Fix* names six sites: four live ones (ARCHITECTURE, the constant, both READMEs)
*"and correct the two narrative ones"* — `docs/ROADMAP.md` (the round-7 ledger block) and
`docs/DEVLOG.md`. The closure note says:

> **All four live sites** now publish the measured spread instead of the unreachability claim …

`git show e704ce6 -- docs/ROADMAP.md` touches *"What is left before the tag"* and the round-8 ledger
block. **The round-7 block is untouched**, and at HEAD `docs/ROADMAP.md:2120-2132` reads, unmarked and
in the present tense:

> … the largest phone-bearing request the proxy accepts (**16 MiB, 221,941 numbers**) spends
> **221,941 of 500,000**. *A legal phone-bearing body cannot reach the allowance at all*;
> `MAX_BODY_BYTES` binds first. What reaches it is text that **fails** validation …
>
> … Idle, the slowest legal body is that 16 MiB result at **2.56 s** and every refusal lands at
> ~1.4–1.9 s.

Every sentence there is refuted by the block twenty lines below it. A reader scanning M10's ledger for
*"what does the budget do?"* meets the retracted claim first, with nothing marking it.

**The DEVLOG site is different and should be left alone.** DEVLOG is explicitly chronological
(*"Newest first. One entry per meaningful change"*), so a round-7 entry stating what round 7 concluded
is the file working as designed, and the round-8 entry above it carries the correction. ROADMAP is not
that file — CLAUDE.md says it *"stays forward-looking"* and is the ledger. So the finding is about one
of the two sites, not both; **but the closure note asserted completion over a list of six after
touching four**, which is M10-R51's own prescription (*"a closure note must quote the finding's site
list and mark each one"*) not being applied.

**Fix.** Mark the round-7 block rather than rewrite it — one line is enough, and preserving what the
round concluded is the point of a ledger: *"(Retired by [M10-R53](reviews/M10.md#m10-r53) and
[M10-R56](reviews/M10.md#m10-r56): a legal phone-bearing body does reach the allowance — the grouped
one-column line is ~793 KB.)"* And when closing this round, quote the site list and mark every entry,
including the ones deliberately left alone and why.

**Closed (2026-07-30).** The ROADMAP''s round-7 ledger block no longer asserts the refuted claim, and
the DEVLOG''s round-7 entry carries the correction beside it rather than instead of it — history keeps
what was believed, the ledger keeps what is true. Both narrative sites were named in
[M10-R53](#m10-r53)''s *Fix* and neither was touched by its closure, which is the same class as
[M10-R58](#m10-r58) and closed in the same pass.

<a id="m10-r61"></a>
### M10-R61 — two mechanical defects introduced by `e704ce6`: a duplicated sentence in ROADMAP and seven doubled apostrophes in the review record · **low (docs)**

Both are artefacts of how the commit was written, not of what it decided.

**(a) `docs/ROADMAP.md:1813-1817` — item 3 of *"What is left before the tag"* carries its last two
lines twice:**

```
3. **Bump `Cargo.toml` to `1.2.1`** — a `chore(release):` commit at tag time, as `1.2.0` was. Left
   at `1.2.0` on purpose: `release-build-publish.yml` refuses to publish a tag that disagrees with
   the manifest, so the mismatch is currently *protective*, not a gap.
   at `1.2.0` on purpose: `release-build-publish.yml` refuses to publish a tag that disagrees with
   the manifest, so the mismatch is currently *protective*, not a gap.
```

Visible in `git show e704ce6 -- docs/ROADMAP.md` as a `+`-only hunk repeating the lines above it. The
content is right — I verified the guard by simulation, and `v1.2.1` is correctly blocked while
`Cargo.toml` says `1.2.0`.

> **(a) is already gone, and by this round's own hand rather than a closure — recorded because a
> reviewer quietly fixing what a reviewer found is exactly the kind of thing that should not be
> silent.** *"What is left before the tag"* had to be rewritten this round (it declared all 55 findings
> closed and named round 9 as the next step), and the duplicated pair sat inside the block being
> replaced. There was no version of that edit that preserved it. The finding stays on the record with
> its repro pinned to `e704ce6` so the mechanism is not lost; **only (b) is left to fix.**

**(b) `docs/reviews/M10.md` lines 4452, 4457, 4462, 4519, 4520, 4522, 4526 — seven `''` where `'` is
meant** (`maintainer's call`, `DOS-BUD's`, `TESTING's`, `milestone's`, `DOS-09''s`), all inside
R53's and R54's closure notes: a shell-quoting escape that survived into the file. (Line 1130's `''`
is legitimate — it denotes an empty `PII_LOCALES`.)

**Fix.** Delete the duplicated two lines; replace the seven `''` with `'`.

**Test that would have caught it.** Nothing mechanical is worth building for this. It is recorded
rather than silently fixed because both are **diff-visible**: a `+`-only hunk repeating the lines
above it, and a same-line character substitution. *Re-reading the rendered diff of a docs-only commit
catches both in seconds* — M10-R51's discipline for site lists, applied to the commit itself.

**Closed (2026-07-30).** The duplicated lines are gone from `What is left before the tag`, and the
seven doubled apostrophes are gone from this file.

Both were introduced by *how* the commit was written rather than by what it decided: the doubled
apostrophes are PowerShell single-quote escaping leaking through a scripted edit, and the duplication
is a splice that overlapped its own tail by two lines. Worth one line of record because the mechanism
is specific and will recur — **a scripted edit to prose needs the same read-back a scripted edit to
code gets from the compiler**, and prose has no compiler.
